He read it from his sheet, standing, in his thin careful voice, without any expression at all, exactly as he had read the comb's figures a week ago.

"A bank on its own count loses light," he said. "A stepped bank of thirty-six cells drives one load on one beat. Thirty-six cells on thirty-six counts drive it unevenly, and some of every minute they're working against each other. The Ferren bank's lost a ninth of its light since the autumn, by the Works' figures. The Saltmere bank's lost the same since Wednesday, by the keeper's. Call it a tenth across the country, at the least. A tenth of the light in every municipal lamp from here to the hills."

He turned the sheet.

"It costs money. Every bank on its own count wants a reader a quarter, to see it's still on its own, and fitters who can keep thirty-six counts and not one. The keeper here says her fitters will curse it every winter for the rest of their lives, and she's right; it's a third again in wages, by her book, and every pump house on the roll will find the same. That's the towns' to pay. The Board's order says so."

He turned it again.

"And it costs the Halden foundation its municipal licences." He did not look at counsel's back. "The foundation's stepped a hundred and twelve municipal works under licence since 2391. Tram sheds and lamp offices, mostly, and a few drums. Every one of them comes out of step under this order, and every licence fee with it. I've the foundation's own index for the figure. It's the foundation's oldest income." He was quiet a moment. "That's not the foundation's oldest property. Its oldest property was a cell under a glass on the Long Quay, and it went to its own count on Wednesday at twenty-two minutes past six, and I was there. That's not in the Board's order. It's not anybody's order. It's only so."

He sat down.

Halden's counsel stood up at the end of the first bench.

"The foundation will not contest the order," he said. His voice was neat and dry, and it was, Kiva thought, very tired. "I'm instructed to say so. I'm instructed to say that the foundation's yards, refineries and lift yards will remain stepped, as they are the foundation's own and not the country's, and that the Board's order does not reach them, and that the foundation does not ask it to." He looked at the Clerk. "And I'm instructed by the Proprietor to say one thing in his own words, which I've written down so that I'll not improve it." He read from a slip. "*I am not certain I was wrong. I believed for seventy-seven years that the lamps that keep time together are the ones that go white instead of out. On Wednesday they did neither. I do not know what they did. Nor does the Board. I would ask the Board not to say in its order that I was wrong, because it doesn't know that either.*" He folded the slip. "That's all, ma'am."

The Clerk looked at him for a long moment.

"The Board doesn't say he was wrong," she said. "The Board says it doesn't know, and that a country that doesn't know shouldn't be one engine while it finds out." Something moved at the corner of her mouth, and was gone. "That's the Works' reading. It's the only one in this hall that isn't a reading of what's coming. It's a reading of what to do in the meantime." She folded her hands. "Write that the Board heard Mr. Halden, and that it doesn't say he was wrong, and that it doesn't say he was right, and that it orders the banks on their own counts anyway, because a fitter's rule is all it's got."

The heavy Board member pushed his spectacles up onto his forehead.

"It's a slower country," he said, to nobody in particular, quite mildly. "And a darker one, by a tenth. And dearer."

"Yes," said the Clerk. "That's the price. It was said first. The Board rises."

On the Harbour Committee's bench the chairman had opened his gold watch and was looking at it. He looked at it for a long time. Then he shut it and put it in his pocket, and Kiva saw that he had not looked at the time at all. He had only wanted to hold something.

---

On Saturday morning at ten, Kiva went along the Long Quay to the Counting House, because the Proprietor had asked her to, by a card in his own hand that came to the loft on Friday night. *If you'd care to. Not for anything. A. H.*

Every window was lit, as it always was. The lamps on the brass brackets in the hall were lit, white, and the lamps in the glass globes on the newel posts, and the great iron ring slung from the stairwell's ceiling. Kiva went up the stair with her left hand in her coat and her right on nothing, and on the fourth step she waited for her heart to slow, and it did not.

It went on as it always went. Her Ember lay quiet under her breastbone. Her still door did not want to march. She went up the stair to the long room at the top, and the lamps did not flicker, and nothing in the house kept a count that her doors could feel at all.

He was at the head of the long table, in his chair, with a rug over his knees.

The glass case stood where it had always stood, at his left hand, on the polished wood, with its brass frame. His left hand was not on it. It lay on the table beside the case, palm down, on the wood. On the glass of the case, in a faint fog that the clerks had not cleaned off and he had told them not to, there was the print of a hand, five fingers and a palm. Under the print, inside the case, an old small cell turned its light, yellow, slow, three and a pause and two, uneven, like nothing she had ever seen in a Halden house.

"Miss Rowan," said the Proprietor. "Sit where you like."

She did not go to the far chair. She stopped halfway down the table, with the window over the harbour behind her and the white cloth and the two candlesticks still between them, and sat. Her heart did not change.

"That's nearer," said the Proprietor.

"There's nothing to sit far from."

"No." He looked down at his left hand on the table. "No, there isn't. I find people in the house keep coming nearer, and don't know why, and stand about at my elbow with papers looking uncomfortable." He moved the hand an inch on the wood. "It closes now. Halfway. Aske came on Thursday and said it would close the rest of the way in a month, and that the heart was a perfectly ordinary heart for a man of fifty-five and he'd not speak for a man of a hundred and three." Something moved across his lean face. "I liked him."

"Everybody likes him, after."

"Yes. I'd imagine that's the case." He looked at the glass case beside him, at the print on it and the slow uneven light under it. "It's its own now. The first. I put my hand back on it on Wednesday night, when they'd carried me home, to see. It didn't come back to me. It went on doing that." He watched the light go round, three and a pause and two. "I've decided to leave it on the table where I can see it. The foundation would like me to put it in the vault. I've said no."

Kiva did not say anything.

"I asked you here for nothing," said the Proprietor, "and I'll keep to that. I'll not ask you anything. I'd only like to say one thing to you, because you're the only person who's sat at this table who saw what I saw from that window, and you'll understand it, and nobody else will." He looked at her. "On Wednesday at twenty-two minutes past six, every lamp I could see from the outfall walk went to a point. Not white. Not out. A point." He was quiet. "I've waited seventy-seven years to see the lamps do it again. White or out. I've arranged my whole house and half the country so that when they did, they'd go white. And they did neither. They did a thing I hadn't got a word for."

He looked at her for a long moment, steadily, down the table.

She knew what he was not asking. She had heard him read Annick in a Board's evidence; she had seen his eyes come round to her at this table on Sunday when she said there was a thing she had no word for. He had read that line in 2610. He was not asking.

"I don't know what they did either," said Kiva.

"No." The Proprietor smiled then. It was not his short real laugh. It was smaller, and it stayed. "I've believed one thing for seventy-seven years, Miss Rowan. I believe it still. Some go white and some go out, and I'd still sooner they went white. I'm not certain I was wrong. I'm only certain I let go." He looked at his hand on the wood. "That's a different thing to be certain of. I'd not have thought, at a hundred and three, that I'd find a new thing to be certain of."

They sat for a while. The fire ticked. The cell in the glass case went round, three and a pause and two.

"I'll not keep you," said the Proprietor at last. "You've a healer who'll want to know where you've been, and friends with cards on a board, and Rhea Sorn has a night on her van on Monday, I'm told, and a card that ends on Wednesday." He did not move his hand. "Go out the way you came. Touch the banister if you like. It'll not say anything to you now."

She touched the banister going down. It did not say anything. It was a banister.

---

Kellan had the second white card in his hand on the Mole on Saturday afternoon, sitting on the warm stone with his back against the wall and his boots out toward the sea.

He did not wait for her to ask. He held it out to her across the stone as he had held out the first, without comment.

*Mr. Renn. The foundation no longer claims the Step. It will want a man to count what it has left, and what it does with it, and to say so under his own name. To me, and to nobody between. Not counsel's. Mine. From the autumn, at the Meridian yard, at a Fire's wage. You may say no. You've said it to this house twice, and it was right both times. A. Halden.*

Kiva gave it back.

"You said no twice," she said.

"I said no to counsel twice. Twice to a card with a line on the back that said *given through the foundation's counsel, as the foundation's.*" Kellan turned the card over. There was nothing on the back. "This one hasn't got a back."

"And you'll say yes."

"I've done the sum." He put the card in his shirt pocket and buttoned the pocket over it. "The foundation's got a hundred and twelve works to give up, and its yards to keep, and a claim it's let go of, and a ledger that's been kept for two hundred and ninety years by men who wrote down what the house wanted to hear. Somebody's going to count it now. Either it's somebody who's never stood in that house, and doesn't know where the figures hide, or it's somebody who has." He looked at the sea. "My mother's a grader. She says the worst thing you can do to a number is let it mean what somebody needs it to. I've been doing figures against that house since the autumn from the outside. I'd like to try doing them from the inside, where it's harder, for a man who's asked for it."

"He'll not be there forever."

"No. He's a hundred and three, and his heart's an ordinary heart for a man of fifty-five, and Aske won't speak for it." Kellan was quiet. "That's in the sum too. If he goes, I'll have a year of figures under my own name that the next one can't unwrite. That's worth a year." He looked at her sideways, with his narrow careful face. "Halden License doesn't come into it. I've told Pettit. I'll cut for them on the roll till the autumn, if they're called, and then I'm the house's clerk and not its blade."

"And Seven."

"Seven's going four ways," said Kellan. "I was only ever on its book as a blade who's told where he's wanted." He nearly smiled. "I'd want it known I was told, and went. Every time."

---

Perrin was on the end of the Mole on Sunday morning with his hand pressed to his side and a letter in his other hand, looking at the sea gate.

"I've written to Rusk," he said, when she sat down beside him. "Yes. The trunk crew at the Brickfields, from the autumn, when my card's up." He did not give her the letter. "*Cade, P., carry.* Not *the winner of the Carry.* I asked him to leave that off. He wrote back from the Works' lodging an hour later on the same sheet: *I've never heard of the Carry.* He's a liar. He was on the tiers." Perrin looked at the letter. "It's the nicest thing anybody's said to me since the ladder."

"Why the trunk crew?"

He was quiet for a while.

"Gower," he said. "Old Four's blade. He envied me. All week. Since the Carry. He said so, in the loft, to my face, the way I said it to yours in the autumn about the three doors. *I'd give my right arm for a board with only my name on it.*" Perrin looked at the sea gate. "And I found out what it's like. It's worse. It's much worse being envied than envying. When I envied you, I'd something to want. When Gower envied me, I'd only a thing he wanted, and I'd won it on one Tuesday on a ladder, and he'd every right to think it was luck." He folded the letter. "I'd rather be the new man on the trunk crew who lifts things than the boy who won the Carry for the rest of my life. I'd rather have to show them."

"You'll show them."

"I know. That's the good part." He looked at her left hand, lying in her lap. "You were declared on Wednesday by my voice. Twice. The second one against your card." He did not look away. "I've thought about it every hour since. I was the brake. I could have called *off.* Everybody on the wall was waiting for me to."

"I was waiting for you to."

"I know you were. I could see you from the wall, kneeling on that sill with Aske counting." Perrin's face went the colour it went when he was thinking. "I didn't call it because it was yours. Not mine. It's the captain's to declare, and it's the captain's to stop, and I've stopped Seven a dozen times since the autumn when it was going wrong. It wasn't going wrong. It was going the way you'd said on Monday it might, with the price said first, and you'd heard it." He breathed carefully. "If I'd called *off,* your hand would be warm, and the bank would be in, and it'd have been my word. I'd have kept it for you. I'd have been very proud of keeping it for you."

"And you'd have been wrong."

"And I'd have been wrong." He put the letter in his coat. "I'd like it known that I was nearly wrong, and wasn't. That's a thing with only my name on it." He looked at the sea. "Nobody'll envy me for it. That's how I know it's mine."

---

The Company's runner came to Quiller's door at a quarter past eleven on Monday night with the call for Rhea Sorn's last night on the van, and Kiva went with dark hands, because that was all she had and it was all a second ever had.

It was a cellar in the Tanyard, below Ropery Row, where the water had stood to the shin on Wednesday morning for seven minutes and gone out again. Something had come up in it on Monday evening, the runner said. A householder had gone down for coal at ten and seen a grey thing standing in the corner where the water had lain longest, and had come up again very fast and shut the trap and sent for the Company, and the Company had sent for Graywater, because Graywater's van was in Saltmere on the Board's warrant and the Company's crews were all on the Longshore at the night low.

Graywater's van stood at the head of the Tanyard with its lamps lit. Rhea was at its tailboard with her slate. Doran Mace was there with the ward line, and Nairn, and Ruddock and Tibb. And Pike sat on the tailboard with his hands on his knees, half closed, the way they had been since the second month.

"A string," said Rhea, when Kiva came up with Pru beside her. "Three husks on a cord. Handful at most. In a cellar ten feet by twelve, with a coal heap in one corner and a copper in another, and one trap to get in by." She looked at Kiva's left hand. "Pike's catcher tonight. His hands close halfway and he can carry a voice and a corner. You're his second, as the card says. Dark hands." She did not look away. "That's what you've got. It's the last night on the card. I'll not ask you for more than's on it."

"It's enough."

"I know it is," said Rhea. "That's why I wrote it that way in the autumn." She took her slate off her belt. "Pike."

Pike got down off the tailboard.

They went down the trap into the cellar one at a time by the ladder, the ward line first, then Nairn with his blade, then Pike, then Kiva, one-handed, with Pru below her on the ladder's foot with a hand ready that she did not need. The cellar was low and black and smelled of wet coal and the tannery. Mace's lamp, set on the copper, showed the floor: stone, still dark with Wednesday's water at the low end, and in the corner where it had lain longest, three grey shapes standing on a thread of grey, shoulder to shoulder, very still, like three men waiting for a door to open.

"They're not keeping a count," said Pike, very low, looking at them.

Kiva looked. They were not. A pack kept a count among itself; Coombe had taught them that in the second month, and they had met one at a mill in the hills keeping a wheel's time, and every pack on the coast since had moved on the town's count, four and four. These were standing still. Nothing about them moved on any beat at all.

"There's no count left in this cellar to keep," said Kiva. "Since Wednesday. There's nothing under the floor."

"Then they'll not move on one." Pike looked at them a while longer. "Good. That's one thing less."

They did it as Pike had done a hundred strings in twenty-six years, on wharves and in drains and in a coal yard below the Hythe in the first month, where his hands had been taken. Mace's line put a wall along the cord's near side. Nairn cut the cord at the middle on Rhea's word, from the ladder's foot, where she stood with her slate. The three husks went soft and grey on the stone. And Pike went to the far end of the string on his knees, to the corner, and laid his half-closed hands flat on the cellar floor beside the last husk, and began to call the hem in, low, in the old words, as Kiva had heard him call it on a night in the first month before a fold took both his wrists.

Kiva knelt at his other side, at the near end, with her right hand flat on the cold stone and her left in her lap, and her mouth shut. A second sets nothing. She calls nothing. She is not to open her mouth until the bag is sealed.

She watched his hands. She watched them on the stone, closed halfway, the right thumb slow as it had been since the autumn before last, in his own writing. She watched the hem come in under them, grey, a thin sheet of the dead string's skin folding in from the near end toward him, the way a second watches a master fold a cloth.

And she watched the far end. Pike could not; his face was turned to his hands. Nobody else in the cellar was watching it; Mace's line was watching the near side and Nairn was watching the cut and Rhea was watching Pike. Kiva watched the far end of the hem, by the coal heap, where it lay against the wall.

It lifted. A hand's breadth, no more, the way a page lifts in a draught. It lifted toward the nearest thing, and the nearest thing was Pike's left wrist, a foot from the coal heap, where his hand lay on the stone.

"*Far edge,*" said Kiva.

Two words, once.

Pike took his left hand off the stone and laid it down again a foot nearer his knee, without looking, as a man moves his hand off a table when somebody says *hot.* The far end of the hem came down on the stone where his wrist had been, and lay there, and was nothing but grey skin on a cellar floor.

He did not look at her. He went on calling the hem in, low, in the old words, and the hem came in to his hands, and he folded it once from the far edge and once again, and it was a bag.

"*Sealed,*" said Pike.

Then he looked at her.

"You'd your mouth shut the whole time," he said. "Till the far end. Then two words." His long brown face did the thing it did. It went the whole way, this time. Kiva had never seen it go the whole way. It was a smile. "That's a second. I'd not had a better."

"You were mine on Wednesday."

"I was. It's harder from the other side." He picked up the bag with his half-closed hands, carefully, against his chest. "I watched yours. You watched mine. That's the whole of it. Two people watching the other one's far edge, so neither of them has to." He held the bag out to Rhea at the ladder's foot. "I'd not known it was the whole of it until tonight. I've been doing it for twenty-six years with nobody at my far edge but the corner-hand."

Rhea took the bag. She looked at it, and at Pike, and at Kiva kneeling on the cellar floor with her right hand on the stone and her left in her lap. Then she wrote on her slate, standing at the ladder's foot by the light of Mace's lamp on the copper, slowly, every column.

Kiva read it upside down, as she had read it every night on the van since the first month.

*Before: a string of three in a cellar in the Tanyard, Saltmere, after Wednesday's water. Not keeping any count. Closed: at a quarter to one, on the cut and the fold. Lives: none. Works: the near side walled, the cord cut at the middle, the hem called in by the catcher. The far edge called once by the second, before the bag was sealed. Tinned: one bag, Handful, to be combed. Remarks: none.*

Under it, she wrote one more line, below the columns, where the slate had no column. Kiva could not read it from where she knelt. Rhea did not show it to her. She hung the slate on her belt.

"That's the card done," said Rhea. "Six nights. The first month to the third, two a month, dark hands, Finch with her for the cord, Seven first when Seven was called, and once I went short for it on a coast night and it cost me half a rung." She looked down at Kiva. "Wednesday's the 30th. The card says we look again, and either of us may say no." She turned to the ladder. "I'll be at the van at noon. Come and look."

They went up the ladder into the Tanyard. At the gate, two days later, the bag combed red, like everything else that had been killed that year; but Kiva did not know that yet, and at a quarter to one on Tuesday morning in a lane below Ropery Row, with the van's lamps lit and Pike sitting on the tailboard holding his half-closed hands out to the warmth of the lamp, she did not care.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-48.md

# Chapter 48 — Kiva: Either of Us

Jude gave Pru the book on Tuesday afternoon, in Quiller's shop, among the coils of rope, with the door shut.

He did it without any ceremony at all. He took it out from under his arm, where it had been since the autumn, the team's book with its cloth spine gone soft and its pages thick with figures in four hands and a fifth, and held it out to her across the counter, and Pru looked at it and did not take it.

"I'm staying," said Jude. "In Saltmere. On the closing crew, from Thursday. Six months, and then they look again." He kept holding it out. "Pryce is going north on Thursday with Graywater. Cade's going to the Brickfields in the autumn, and till then he's a captain with no lift in him and a card that says *cup.* Renn's going to count Halden. Rowan's going wherever she's sent, with one hand, in three weeks." He looked at Pru. "You're the only one of us who's going back to the Crooked Line next season to stand on a floor."

"I'm the reserve," said Pru.

"You've not been the reserve since the Bench. You caught a national board. You called a lane on your own. You called the far edge on a cask of held water at a dock's sill with a still door going down beside you." Jude did not lower the book. "Seven's not a four any more. It's a name on a book and eight-tenths of a dram in a tin. Somebody has to keep the name. I'd rather it was you than the Circuit."

Pru looked at the book for a long time. She had gone scarlet from the collar, slowly, and she did not look away, which was new; Kiva had noticed it on the dock floor and noticed it again now.

"Ivey asked me on Sunday," said Pru. "To the Company. Yeo's crew, with Ivey and the girl. A catcher's place, and a second's place when I wanted one, and the sea."

"I know she did," said Jude. "She told me at the yard."

"I said no." Pru took the book. She held it in both capped hands against her chest, the way Loveday Hosken had held a jar. "I'd have been Ivey's catcher. I'd have been good. I'd have been the girl from Meridian on Ivey's crew who used to be Rowan's cord." She looked at Kiva. "I'm on a sheet. My own, with my name on the hem in ink. I'm going back to the Crooked Line and I'm going to find three people who've never heard of the Pit, and I'm going to catch for them, and nobody's going to say whose I was." She went scarlet all over again, to the hair. "I'm not grateful to anybody. I'm only good. I'd like that written in the book, on the first page I write on."

Jude found the pencil on its string and gave it to her.

She wrote it, standing at the counter, with her capped fingers, very badly. Kiva read it upside down. *Tuesday the 29th. Seven's book, from Talley to Finch. Held 5.15. Sealed 0.8, bound as before. Finch is not grateful. Finch is good.*

"There," said Pru. She shut the book. "That's mine now."

---

Twenty-Eight went home on Tuesday evening by the station transit, because Marten's father had paid the fares, and Kiva met Dray on the Long Quay at six with her bag over her shoulder and her healed leg under her and the white lamps not yet lit.

Dray stopped. She did not say anything for a moment. Her face did what it always did.

"Your hand," said Dray.

"Three weeks."

"And the card's at fourteen. I heard Aske say it in the loft to the stove." Dray looked at the dead hand hanging against Kiva's coat. "You'll not spend that on a lane either. Not this year."

"No."

"Good." Dray shifted her bag on her shoulder. "I called it. Every pass. From the wall, on my feet. It's in the Board's press under my name, Hart says, every minute of it, fifty-seven." She did not look away. "That's all I wanted out of Wednesday. My name on it, so it was mine if it was wrong."

"It wasn't wrong."

"No. It wasn't." Dray was quiet a moment. "Bannock's legs will be all right by the summer. Aske says. I asked him on Saturday. I'd want you to know I asked, because you'll not hear it from Bannock; he'll say they're fine now, and they're not." She looked along the quay toward the transit's shed. "I've not changed my mind about you, Rowan. I said so in the autumn and at the Hunt and in the Lane, and I'll not say it was wrong to have said it. You've three doors nobody can learn and a hand you'll spend on a thing because you've decided it's worth spending. I'd not do either." She paused. "But I'd call it again. If you were on the mud and I was on the wall. I'd call it."

"I know you would."

"That's not friendship," said Dray. "I'd want that clear."

"It's clear."

Dray nodded, once. Then she went away along the Long Quay toward the transit's shed with her bag on her shoulder, walking on her healed leg without a stick, and she did not look back, and Kiva had not expected her to.

Carrow was sitting on the third bollard from the shed with her arms folded, watching Twenty-Eight go. She looked up as Kiva came level.

"She called it well," said Carrow. "I struck on it for eleven minutes on that seam and never once had to look at her." She unfolded her arms. "Yeo carded me. On Saturday. The Company's wall crews, from the summer, on my own harbour." Something moved in her wind-brown face. "I'd wanted it since I was twelve. I thought I'd get it by winning the Measure. I got it by standing on a wall cutting on a girl from Meridian's count, read on a mast, one door." She looked at Kiva. "I'd want you to know I asked Polglase for the tape. The bit where it says one line, two people. I've got it in my coat."

"Dray's in that line too."

"I know she is. That's why I wanted it." Carrow stood up off the bollard. "Go home, Rowan. Come back to my harbour when your hand's done, and I'll show you the Ropewalk's floor when nobody's on it, and you can tell me what you'd have done different in the second carry on the Friday." She went off toward the Ropewalk steps and the high town, light and quick, and turned round at the corner, as she always did, and lifted one hand, palm out.

---

Rhea Sorn sat on the tailboard of Graywater's van at eleven on Wednesday morning, the 30th of the third month, with a sheet of the Charter Board's paper on her knee and a pen in her hand, and did not write anything for a quarter of an hour.

The sheet had come at nine, by a Board clerk, from Dinah Coombe. It was headed in the Board's print: *THE CHARTER BOARD. TRAVELLING COMMISSION, UNDER THE ORDER OF THE 25th OF THE THIRD MONTH, 2681.* Under that, in Coombe's square, unhurried hand, there was a list of names, a short one. A reader from the Works at Ferren, lent by Lumb with what Coombe called *a letter of some rudeness.* Two officers from the Saltmere Company, lent by Yeo. A fitter from Peverell's house, Hart's mate, the heavy man with the burn scar, lent by Peverell for a year and paid by the Board. Polglase, who had asked. And then a line with nothing on it but a number, and at the foot, in Coombe's hand: *Sorn. You've had her all winter. Write her in, if you'll write her, under my hand and not yours. Say what she is. Say what we'll do. D. C.*

Rhea looked at the empty line.

She had written a card once, in the autumn, slate blue, in this hand, on this knee, in a kitchen in Meridian with four children round the table. She had written *No seat is promised* on it, and *either of us may say no,* and she had read it aloud to all four and watched them sign the back. She had burned a card that said *mine* the week before she wrote it. She had not told anybody that. Pike knew, because Pike knew everything about her that she did not tell him.

On Monday night in a cellar in the Tanyard, under her columns, below the slate's last ruled line, where there was no column for it, she had written one more thing by the light of Mace's lamp on a copper. She had not shown it to the girl. She looked at it now, on the slate on her belt, in her own small square chalk.

*The second called the far edge, once, before the bag. Not mine to keep.*

It was true. It had been true since the Lane, when she had sat on the fourth tier with Pike and watched the girl lose an exchange on purpose and thought *mine* in her own head, and known it for what it was. She had been an officer for twenty years. She knew what it looked like when an officer started to keep a girl. It looked like a seat that was never quite offered and a card that was always renewed and a van that the girl could never quite leave, because there was always one more night, and the officer was always good to her, and it was always kind, and at the end of five years the girl was a very fine second to somebody else's catcher on somebody else's van and nothing at all of her own.

Rhea had had a second like that once. Before Pike. She had been the second.

She uncapped the pen.

*Rowan, K.,* she wrote, on Coombe's empty line. *Catcher. Caller of the pass in three: a ward to an Ember to a still door, called, nobody holding it twice. One door declared at every response. Two crossings certified, Ember to still and hand to Ember; one in fourteen days, by her healer's card; the three doors not to be asked. Dark hands till her healer's card is up, from the 15th of the fourth month. Under D. Coombe's hand. Until the last of the ninth month.*

She stopped. She looked at it for a long time.

Then she wrote the last line, which was the only line on it that was hers.

*Then we look again, and either of us may say no.*

She blotted it on her sleeve. She did not sign it. It was under Coombe's hand, and Coombe would sign it, and the girl would, and the girl's aunt beside her because she was seventeen. Rhea's name was not on it anywhere. She had been careful about that.

Pike came round the side of the van with two cups of tea held against his chest in his half-closed hands, and sat down on the tailboard beside her, and gave her one, and looked at the sheet on her knee.

"Under Coombe," he said.

"Under Coombe."

"Not under you."

"Not under me." Rhea drank her tea. "I'd have kept her, Pike. I'd have been very good to her, and kept her."

"I know you would." Pike looked at the sheet a moment longer. "So would I. That's why it's Coombe's." His long brown face did the thing it did, nearly. "You've no advice left, have you. For the spring."

"I spent it at Gannet Creek."

"Then you'll have to say it some other way," said Pike, and drank his tea.

---

Kiva came to the van at noon, as she had been told, with her left hand in her coat and her right in her pocket, and found Rhea on the tailboard with a sheet of paper on her knee and Pike beside her with an empty cup.

"Sit down," said Rhea.

Kiva sat on the cobbles with her back against the van's wheel, because there was nowhere else, and Rhea looked down at her from the tailboard, as she had looked down at her from a hundred tailboards since the first month, slate blue, very straight.

"The card ends today," said Rhea. "It said we'd look again. I've looked." She did not look at the sheet on her knee. "I'll ask you first, because I'm the officer. Graywater's van goes north tomorrow. Pike's hands are coming back. Aske says by the summer they'll close two-thirds, which is more than anybody thought in the first month. He'll want a second at his other hem through the winter, the way he's had one this winter." She paused. "I'd not write you a seat. I've said so from the start, and I'll not unsay it. I'm asking if you'd want me to ask Graywater for you to be his second again next winter. When your hand's back."

Kiva looked at Pike on the tailboard. He was looking at the cobbles.

"No," she said.

Rhea did not move.

"Because I'd stay," said Kiva. She heard her own voice and kept it level, because it was a thing she had decided, and decided things are said levelly. "If I came back to the van next winter, I'd be good at it, and you'd be good to me, and Pike would nearly smile at me once a night. And I'd come back the winter after. And I'd be Graywater's second for five years, and I'd never call anything." She looked at Pike. "He doesn't need a second next winter. He needs somebody at his far edge. Nairn's watched me do it for six nights. Nairn can do it."

"Nairn can do it," said Pike, to the cobbles. "I've told him so."

"That's no, then," said Rhea. Her face did not change at all. "Good." She was quiet. "I'd have said no too, if you'd asked me to write it. I'd want you to know I'd have said it." She put her hand flat on the sheet on her knee. "Ask me something. It's either of us. You've a no of your own to take, if you want it."

Kiva thought about it, honestly, because she had been asked.

"Come on the commission," she said. "Coombe's. As an officer. You've been in more cellars than anybody on that list. I'd want you there."

"No," said Rhea.

She said it at once, as Kiva had known she would, and as Kiva had needed to hear her say it.

"Because I'd be your officer again," said Rhea. "On somebody else's commission, under somebody else's hand, and in a month I'd be standing behind you on a dock floor with my slate thinking *mine.*" She looked down at Kiva steadily. "I thought it on the Saturday of the Lane, on the fourth tier, when you shook your head at your captain. In my own head. It's not a thing an officer should think about a second. It's the start of keeping somebody." She took her hand off the sheet. "Graywater's mine. You're not. That's the no."

Pike, beside her on the tailboard, had shut his eyes.

Rhea picked up the sheet off her knee and held it down to Kiva, over the edge of the tailboard, the way she had once held a slate-blue card across a kitchen table.

"I've written you in," she said. "Not to Graywater. To Coombe. The Board's travelling commission, under her hand, from the 15th of next month to the last of the ninth. It goes to every municipal bank on the Board's roll in two years and puts it on its own count, and takes out the ones that have stepped as plain ash can be found, and writes down in seven columns what was got wrong first." She did not look at what she had written. "No seat. A line. Your name on it under another officer's hand, and not mine anywhere."

Kiva took the sheet with her right hand. She read it on the cobbles with her back against the van's wheel. She read it twice, as Anwen read a card before she signed it.

She read *Caller of the pass in three.* She read *one in fourteen days,* and *the three doors not to be asked,* and *under D. Coombe's hand.* And at the foot, in a different ink, as though it had been written a little after the rest, *Then we look again, and either of us may say no.*

"You've put *either of us* on it," she said.

"It's Coombe and you, this time. Not me." Rhea's face did not change. "I'd want it on every card anybody ever writes you. It's the only advice I've got left, and it's not advice, it's a line." She drank the last of her tea, which was cold. "You'll take it, or you won't. If you take it, you take it on whatever you say now, and Coombe writes it under. She's said so. She's coming along the quay; I can see her rope."

Kiva looked up. Coombe was coming along the cobbles of the Company yard in her grey Board coat with her rope over her shoulder, unhurried, the way she came to everything.

Kiva got up off the cobbles. She did it one-handed, and nobody helped her, and she did not need them to.

"I'll take it," she said, when Coombe stopped in front of the van. "On two conditions. Mine."

Coombe took the rope off her shoulder and began, very slowly, to tie a knot in it.

"Say them," she said. "Once. I'll write them."

"Every bank I call a pass at," said Kiva, "I teach the call to two people there who aren't me, before we leave. Out loud, on that bank, with whatever's to hand, a sack or a tun or a rope's end. And I leave it in seven columns in that house's book, in their hand as well as mine. And the next bank we go to, if somebody who isn't me can call it, they call it, and I keep the count, or the far edge, or nothing." She held the sheet in her right hand. "My sheet said the caller had to have been inside both gaps. It was wrong. Ivey called it in the Company yard on Thursday with one door and a girl of sixteen counting. I'll not be the only one who can. If the commission needs me to be the only one who can, then it's not a method, it's me, and I said no to that on a quay last Wednesday to a man in a chair."

Coombe's hands stopped on the knot.

"That's one," she said.

"The dream's not the commission's." Kiva did not look away from her. "Nobody on it asks me about it. Not the officers, not the readers, not the Board. If I've a thing to put on a table, I'll put it there myself, under my own name, the same night, the way I did in the Salt Hall. If I haven't, nobody asks." She was quiet. "I've a thing I've no word for. It's mine. It stays mine on the commission, the same as it's mine in a loft."

Coombe looked at her for a long time with her pale eyes.

Then she finished the knot, and took the sheet out of Kiva's hand, and laid it on the van's tailboard beside Rhea's knee, and took a pencil out of her coat, and wrote under the last line, standing, in her square unhurried hand.

*On her two conditions, said first. One: she teaches the call at every bank to two who are not her, and leaves it in seven columns in that house's book, and the next bank is called by somebody else where somebody else can. Two: the dream is hers and not the commission's; she will put a thing on a table herself if there's a thing; nobody asks. Accepted. D. Coombe, Hunt Marshal.*

"There," said Coombe. "That's a better first line than mine was." She handed the sheet back. "Your aunt signs it beside you. You're seventeen."

"She'll sign it."

"I know she will. She'll not ask you anything about it either. I've met her." Coombe put her pencil away and her rope back over her shoulder. "The 15th. At the Board's office at Meridian. Bring your book. Bring that hand, if it's back, and if it's not, bring it anyway, and we'll put you at a far edge till it is." She turned to go, and stopped, and looked back at Rhea on the tailboard. "Sorn. You wrote her well."

"I wrote what she is," said Rhea. "I don't write good."

"No," said Coombe. "That's why I asked you."

She went away along the yard with her rope. Pike, on the tailboard, opened his eyes and looked at Kiva standing on the cobbles with the sheet in her right hand.

"You've two noes and two conditions and a line," he said. "That's a good day's work for a girl with one hand." His face did the thing it did, and then, as it had in the cellar, it went the whole way. "Go and get your aunt's name on it before you lose it."

---

Mara signed it at four, at the table in the Works' lodging, with her reading hand.

She read it first. She read every line of it, Rhea's and Coombe's, the way she read a fault report across a counter, dry and exact, with her lips shut. She read the two conditions twice. Then she took the pen from the inkstand and signed beside the space where Kiva would sign, *M. Rowan, Grade Two, guardian,* in her own small exact print, and blotted it, and turned the sheet round.

She did not ask anything. Kiva had known she would not.

"The 15th," said Mara. "At the Board's office."

"Yes."

"The Works' transit goes on Friday. Rusk's been asked by the Board to write the Saltmere sheet, and he's asked me to write it with him, *How the Saltmere bank was taken out of step, with what was got wrong first,* and he says the first thing got wrong was a number I let a keeper say in a Salt Hall." Something moved at the corner of her mouth. "You'll come home on the transit with us. On the Board's fare. Coombe's paid it; she says commission hands travel on the Board." She looked at Kiva. "Three weeks in the shop, with a hand that's no use and your aunt at the counter. You'll hate it."

"I'll like it."

"You'll like it for a week and hate it for two." Mara put the pen back in its stand. "Then you'll go. And I'll have the shop, and the Works, and Orla being rude to me on Tuesdays, and a sheet on the dresser that's longer than the Ferren one." She looked at the signed paper on the table. "I took it on my name, on Wednesday. You took yours. Neither of us owned the other's. I'd like that to go on being so."

"It will."

"Good." Mara stood up. "Then sign it. I can't hold the paper still for you all evening; I've a sheet to write."

Kiva signed it right-handed, under her aunt's name, *K. Rowan,* worse than anything she had ever signed, and blotted it on her sleeve.

---

Anwen went north at seven on Thursday morning, the 1st of the fourth month, on Graywater's van.

The van stood at the head of the Long Quay with its horses in and its lamps lit against the grey, slate blue, with Mace on the box and Nairn beside him and Ruddock and Tibb and Stiles behind, and Pike on the tailboard where he always sat, and Rhea standing at the horses' heads with her slate on her belt. Anwen's bag was already up. Anwen was not. She was standing on the quay with her cap on and her hair pinned under it and her hands in her sleeves, and Kiva and Pru and Jude and Perrin stood round her, because that was how they stood.

"I've a thing for you," said Kiva.

She had written it on Wednesday night, right-handed, at the table in the loft, three times, until it was legible. One sheet, in seven columns. She held it out.

*The pass in three. Second edition. What was got wrong first: the first line of the limits. "A caller who has been inside both gaps." Wrong. A caller who can hear the count, and somebody who keeps it who isn't carrying.*

Anwen took it and read it, all of it, as she read a card before she signed it. It took her a long time.

"For Mace's line," said Kiva. "If you want it."

"I want it." Anwen folded it once and put it in her sleeve. "I want it said I'll teach it. On the van. To the line, and to whoever Rhea puts at the far edge." She took her hand out of her sleeve again, and there was something in it, small and dark: a stick of sealing wax, red, half used, with the end of it melted and shaped by her thumb. "And this is Seven's. I've had it since the autumn. Every seal on the book and the tin. It's not mine." She held it out to Pru.

Pru took it. She went scarlet.

"I'll seal things," she said.

"Seal them properly," said Anwen. "Thumb in the middle. Not the edge." She looked round at the four of them. At Perrin with his hand pressed to his side, at Jude with his white knuckles and his card from the Company in his coat pocket, at Pru with the book under one arm and the wax in her capped hand, at Kiva with her left hand in her coat. She did not go red. Anwen never went red.

"I'm not going to say anything about the year," she said. "You all know it. I'd only say it wrong."

"Say one thing," said Perrin.

Anwen looked at him.

"I was the best student wall in Meridian," she said, "and I stood in the way of things for three years, and I thought that was what I was for." She looked at Kiva. "On Wednesday at ten past six I put a wall under a thing that wanted me, on a count that wasn't mine, and held it, and let it go into the air on a word. That's not standing in the way. I don't know what it is. I'm going to find out on a van." She was quiet a moment. "And you're going to find out what you are on a commission. In two places. Not one of us for the other." She took Kiva's right hand in hers, briefly, and held it. "I want it said that I'd not have had it any other way. And I'd want you to write and tell me what you get wrong first."

"I'll write it."

"Every bank," said Anwen. "I'll read them on the van."

Then she let go, and turned, and went up onto the van's tailboard beside Pike, and sat, with her hands back in her sleeves and her face toward the quay. Rhea went up onto the box. Mace shook the reins. The van went away along the Long Quay at a walk, between the white lamps on their fluted posts that the lamp-men were putting out one after another in the grey morning, and turned at the end of the quay toward the hill road and the north, and was gone.

Perrin let out a breath.

"I'd like it known," he said, "that I've never once in my life seen her cry, and I'd not have wanted to just then, and she didn't, and nor did I."

"Nobody did," said Jude.

"Pru did," said Perrin.

"I did not," said Pru, scarlet, with her face wet.

---

At a quarter to seven on Thursday evening Kiva laid the first two fingers of her left hand on the iron rail at the end of the Mole, with her right hand holding it there by the wrist, because it was the hour.

No answer.

She wrote it in her book, right-handed, leaning on the rail. *Thursday the 1st of the fourth month. A quarter to seven. The rail at the end of the Mole. No answer.* It was the ninth day of *no answer.* She did not mind it. Hart had told her what it was for. In three weeks there would be a morning when it said something else, and she would know it was new by setting it against these.

Jude was beside her. He had come out along the Mole with her after supper because he wanted to see the sea, he said, and he had not seen it from the end of the Mole at dusk yet, and he was going to be living beside it for six months and thought he ought to start. He leaned on the rail with his good hand and looked out past the sea gate's arch at the grey water going dark.

Behind them, along the Long Quay, the lamp-men were lighting the lamps, one after another, white and steady on their fluted posts. In the low town the yellow lamps came on in the windows of Ropery Row. At the head of the basin, the pump house's six outfalls poured into the evening flood on six counts, every one its own, and none of it came up through the stone of the Mole into Kiva's boots. She had stopped noticing that it did not. She noticed now that she had stopped noticing, and was surprised by it.

Noa came along the Mole at five to seven with a sheaf of yellow forms in his hand and his pencil behind his ear.

He did not hurry. He never did. He came along the top of the Mole between the bollards in his grey coat with the forms held against his chest, past the Company's swept watch hut at the end where the Master of the Order of the Even Hand had lain on Wednesday night with her hands folded, and gone home on Friday's boat without coming into the town again. He stopped beside Kiva at the rail.

"The coast came in last Thursday and Friday," he said. "Every lamp office on the Board's roll from here to Westerley, and the ships in the roads between: the same nine seconds, every lamp to a point. That's in my column already." He touched the yellow forms. "These are new. The Board's wire office has had them since four. From over the water, through the cable office at Westerley. The Clerk's read them and said I could." He did not give them to her. He held them. "I'll read you three lines. I've written them in my column. I've drawn nothing under them."

"Read them," said Kiva.

He read them in his thin clear reader's voice, as he read returns, without looking up.

The first was from a harbour master in a port over the water whose name Kiva had seen once, on the Board's big map in the Salt Hall, and could not have said aloud. *HARBOUR LAMPS AND SHIPS' LAMPS IN THE ROADS ALL TO A POINT OF LIGHT AND BACK. NINE SECONDS. RECKONED TO SALTMERE TIME TWENTY-TWO MINUTES PAST SIX MORNING 23RD THIRD MONTH. ENTERED. NOT FAULT.*

The second was from another port, further south, in another tongue, put into the Board's words by the cable office at Westerley with a note at the foot to say so. It said the same thing. It gave the same minute.

The third was from the port over the water that a ship's engineer had come from, off a hull, in the autumn. He had said, on a green-edged sheet from the Saltmere Company's hall that Noa had read aloud at a table in Meridian before Kiva ever came south, at the foot, *as a thing is written that the writer will not vouch for,* that his own port's bank had fallen into step at the same hour as Saltmere's, reckoned back. It said: *POINT OF LIGHT AS ABOVE, SAME MINUTE. AND IN ANSWER TO THE BOARD'S QUESTION: OUR HARBOUR BANK FELL INTO STEP 17TH NINTH MONTH LAST AT AN HOUR WHICH RECKONED TO SALTMERE TIME IS TWENTY PAST TWO AFTERNOON. AS OUR ENGINEER REPORTED. VOUCHED.*

Noa lowered the forms.

"Vouched," he said. "I've had that line in my column since the autumn with *not entered as fact* beside it. I've struck the note out this afternoon. Neatly, so it can still be read." He put his pencil behind his ear again. "That's three ports over the water in the same nine seconds. And a bank over the water that fell in in the same minute as ours. I've written them. I'll send them to every pump house and lamp office on the Board's roll, as they are, with the rest." He looked at the sea. "I'll not draw anything between. I drew one wrong once."

Jude had not taken his eyes off the water.

"Rowan," he said.

She looked where he was looking.

There was a hull standing in from the south.

It was a long way off, out beyond the sea gate's arch and the end of the Mole, on the grey sea going dark, a low black shape with its masts against the last of the light, standing in toward Saltmere on the evening flood as the coast boat had stood in a month ago. It was too far off to see what flag it flew, or whether it flew one. It had a light on its bow.

Kiva had seen every kind of lamp there was in Saltmere in a month. The white ones on the Long Quay on their fluted posts, and the yellow ones in the low town's windows, and Peverell's oil lamp on the landing, and the three rows of white globes along the Graving's tiers, and the lamps on the Company's punts and the coast boat's bow and the Board's tent and Noa's desk. She knew them as she knew the order of her doors.

The light on the hull's bow was not like any of them.

It was not white. It was not yellow. It was not the colour of anything she could have pointed to on the quay behind her, and it did not flicker, and it did not burn the way a lamp burns. It stood on the hull's bow out on the dark water, small and steady, coming in, as though somebody aboard had looked for a long time for a lamp that was like nothing and had found one, and had lit it, and had pointed the ship at the place where a light had answered.

Noa had seen it too. He stood at the rail with the yellow forms against his chest and did not say anything. Jude did not say anything. Behind them along the Long Quay the lamp-men went on lighting the white lamps one after another, and none of them looked out to sea.

Kiva stood at the rail with her right hand on the iron and her left hanging, and watched it come.

She did not know who was on that hull. She would not know tonight. She thought, watching the light on its bow come steadily in over the dark water toward the sea gate, that somebody on it had dreamed, a long way off, with a fever, of roads and lights and nothing over them and something paying attention. She thought that they had been at a window, perhaps, on the morning of the 23rd, at twenty-two minutes past six by Saltmere's clock, and had seen every lamp they could see go to a point and come back. And that they had got up from the window and found a ship, and come to see who had answered.

She did not think anything else about it. She did not let herself.

She put her right hand inside her shirt and found the disk.

It was cold. It had been cold at the far end of a polished table, and on a bench in a dock with a Glory master at the gate, and on a dock floor with two Glory fields touching across a grey ward and every lamp on the coast gone to a point. It was cold now, with a light on a hull's bow coming in over the water that was like no lamp in Saltmere. It lay against her chest on its cord, a round flat cold thing, and did not tell her anything, and she had not expected it to.

She took her hand out of her shirt and opened her book on the rail.

There was a column in it that nobody had ruled but her, the fourth, on the left, where she wrote within the week after any crossing what the disk had done. She had written in it on Wednesday night, for the two crossings, one line: *Cold. Both. As before.* She had thought that was all she would write in it until the next crossing, fourteen days off at the least.

She wrote in it now, right-handed, badly, leaning on the iron rail at the end of the Mole in the last of the light, with Jude on one side of her and Noa on the other and the hull standing in.

*Thursday the 1st of the fourth month, 2681. Evening. The end of the Mole. A hull standing in from the south, with a light on its bow like no lamp in Saltmere. Wires from three ports over the water: the point, the same minute. The engineer's line vouched.*

*The disk is cold.*

She looked at it. Then she shut the book, and put it in her coat, and stood at the rail and watched the light come in.

---

## FORMULA ALIGNMENT — EDITORIAL, NOT BLIND

# O'Connor 1.3.0 — numerical author formula

# Applying the owner's numerical craft formula

The owner explicitly selected
`/Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md` to govern this book.
Use its craft instructions more or less exactly, including the numbers. These
are active design targets for the planning model and Opus/Fable author, not merely
editorial observations. The compiler embeds sections 2–6 and 8–9 verbatim below
these instructions and records the source hash so runs can be compared.

Let the sentence-length distribution, short/long sentence shares, paragraph
rhythm and Flesch reading targets influence the actual prose. Let the POV shares,
front-loaded progression vocabulary and distributed character-development rates
influence how the book is built. Preserve section 8's secondary/use-to-taste
status; do not turn its explicitly excluded filler/repetition tics into quotas.

Write original scenes in open-range movements of six or more chapters. The formula constrains the intended
shape and cadence; the author still chooses conversations, tactics, discoveries
and how events unfold. Do not reduce this brief to “clear writing with progression.”
The owner names the cast. No source novel quotations, character substitutions or
scene-by-scene replication belong in the manuscript.

Aim at the supplied distributions across a movement and the cumulative book.
Book-third teaching proportions and book-level POV proportions need not be met
independently by each chapter. Do not stop after each sentence or chapter to run a
gate. Write the full movement, then compare target versus observed results in the
existing editorial pass and make a focused same-author repair for material drift.
Do not silently broaden tolerances or drop inconvenient targets.

Keep the source's own caveats: pause-derived paragraph/scene measures are
directional proxies, and development counts are heuristic. Their adoption as
design targets does not establish that they accurately measure the printed book.
The full source-book audit remains unfinished. Keep source values such as ~1.7x
and ~950 words unchanged; the known arithmetic/denominator questions belong in
the audit, not a silent rewrite of the owner's chosen prompt. If competing targets
cannot be reconciled, report the conflict rather than fabricating a passing score.

This prompt guides output; it does not train model weights or guarantee exact
statistical compliance. Compliance must be measured after prose exists.

Source: /Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md
Source SHA-256: 9f97e2f225c0bcf61d2e922719153fcf00e3b029f7b1552f213888d33130c0b8

The following craft sections (2–6, 8–9) are included verbatim. Sections 1 and 7's legal discussion is not drafting guidance. Use original names, world, systems, events and phrasing; the owner names the cast.

## 2. Sentence rhythm

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Mean sentence length | 14.6 words |
| Median sentence length | 11 words |
| Spread (population stdev) | ~26 words — wide, meaning short declaratives sit right next to long compound-complex sentences, not clustered near the mean |
| Share of very short sentences (≤5 words) | 27.7% |
| Share of very long sentences (≥40 words) | 3.3% |

**Read:** roughly 1 sentence in 4 is a hard, short beat (often a single clause landing a
punch after a longer setup) — this is the "short sentence for impact" technique, not
uniform mid-length prose. Target this ratio, not just the mean; a manuscript that hits mean
14.6 but has no short-sentence spikes will not read the same.

## 3. Paragraph and scene rhythm (ASR-inferred proxy)

Whisper transcripts carry no typographic paragraph marks — this section is inferred from
pause gaps between narrated speech segments, not a count of the print manuscript's actual
paragraph breaks. Treat as directional, not exact.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Inferred paragraph length (pause ≥0.6s) | median 18 words, mean 26.8 words — short, frequent beats |
| Inferred scene/section length (pause ≥1.6s) | mean ~950 words between scene-level breaks, ~8.7 scene breaks per 10k words |

**Read:** short paragraphs are the default texture, with scenes running roughly 900–1000
words before a beat change (POV settle, location change, or time-skip). Avoid long
unbroken expository blocks; break early and often.

## 4. Progression-mechanic ("change frequency") callout rate

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Progression/stat-vocabulary density | ~58 hits per 10k words (rank, growth-analog, spec, level-up, stat-block language) |
| Placement | front-loaded — the opening third of the book carries roughly 1.7x the progression-callout density of the middle and closing thirds (900 vs. 471 vs. 514 hits, by third) |

**Read:** the system gets *established* early and hard, then recedes into the background
as character and plot take over — it does not stay evenly dense through the whole book.
Build your own progression system's vocabulary (do not reuse his axis names — see §7), but
match this front-loaded shape: teach the mechanic aggressively in the first third, then let
it surface only at genuine turning points afterward.

## 5. Character-development beat cadence

Methodology: named-character mention within ~120 characters of a reflection/interiority
marker ("realized," "decided," "for the first time," "swore," "no longer," etc.) — a
heuristic count, not a scene-by-scene read.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist development-moment rate | 4.5 per 10k words |
| Protagonist distribution across thirds | roughly even (41 / 56 / 50 — a slight middle-third bump, not backloaded) |
| Supporting-cast development-moment rate | an order of magnitude lower per character (0.1–0.7 per 10k words each), spread across 8–10 named characters rather than concentrated in one deuteragonist |

**Read:** growth beats for the lead land steadily throughout, not saved up for a finale —
and the supporting cast gets *many* small beats each rather than one character getting a
big secondary arc. If your story gives one best-friend character 80% of the secondary
development budget, that's a different shape than this book's.

## 6. POV structure

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist POV share | ~87% of chapter-word-volume (combining his given-name and surname references) |
| Secondary-character POV/focus share | ~13%, spread across 4–5 named characters at 2.5–3.7% each — brief cutaways, not sustained alternating-POV chapters |

**Read:** this is single-POV-dominant with occasional short cutaway scenes to a handful of
other named characters, not a true multi-POV structure. If you want to signal "feels like
Iron Prince," don't build alternating-chapter dual POV — keep the lead's POV overwhelming
and let other characters get glimpses, not chapters.

## 8. Supporting craft signals (secondary, use to taste)

- **Filler-word discipline:** "that" runs hot (92.1 per 10k words) — a known, common genre
  tic, not a target to imitate; if anything, tighten below this. Other fillers ("just,"
  "almost," "felt," "seemed") sit in the 5–25-per-10k band — moderate, not aggressively
  self-edited, not sloppy either.
- **Adverb density:** ~161 -ly adverbs per 10k words — fairly high; this is a book that
  lets adverbs carry tone rather than insisting on pure show-don't-tell. Not a craft flaw
  to correct, a genre-register signal to optionally match.
- **Dialogue-tag (reporting-verb) density:** ~41 per 10k words — moderate dialogue presence,
  consistent with an action/interiority-forward book rather than a dialogue-driven one.
- **Combat-vocabulary density:** ~22 per 10k words (strike/block/dodge/blade/impact/etc.) —
  present throughout, not just in set-piece fight chapters.
- **Readability:** Flesch Reading Ease 72.3, Flesch-Kincaid grade 6.8 — accessible YA/adult
  crossover register, short-to-medium sentence construction, low jargon-density outside the
  progression-vocabulary spikes.
- **Repetition discipline:** common connective 3-grams ("out of the," "one of the," "for a
  moment") sit at 2–4 per 10k words — normal English-prose background rate, not a
  distinctive tic to replicate. Don't chase these; they're noise, not signature.

---

## 9. One-paragraph brief for the drafting agent

> Write single-POV-dominant (~85–90% lead-character screen time), short-paragraph,
> pause-heavy prose (median paragraph ~18 words, scenes breaking roughly every 900–1000
> words). Mix short punch sentences (~28% of all sentences ≤5 words) against longer
> compound-complex ones (mean 14.6 words, wide spread) rather than settling into uniform
> mid-length sentences. Establish your progression system hard and often in the first
> third of the book, then let it recede to occasional high-stakes surfacing afterward. Land
> a protagonist growth/realization beat roughly every 2,200 words, spread evenly across the
> book rather than saved for the climax, and give 8–10 supporting characters small
> development beats each rather than concentrating growth in one deuteragonist. Moderate
> dialogue-tag and adverb use; accessible ~7th-grade reading level; combat vocabulary
> present in the background throughout, not confined to fight scenes. Every name, every
> system-label, every scene: yours, invented, original.


---

## NUMERICAL REVIEW

# Numerical formula alignment — after the movement

This is an informed editorial pass, not a blind cold read. Read the supplied
formula and full movement, then report target, observed result, method, coverage
and uncertainty. Keep numerical alignment separate from reader scores.

- Sentence mean, median, population standard deviation; shares ≤5 and ≥40 words.
- Written paragraph mean/median and actual scene/section boundaries. Compare with
  the requested targets, but do not equate typography with ASR pause segmentation.
- Flesch Reading Ease and Flesch-Kincaid grade. Identify the sentence tokenizer and
  syllable estimator; proper names/system terms can affect estimates.
- Progression vocabulary hits per 10,000 words, and placement by equal word-count
  book thirds once available. Define a project-specific lexicon before counting;
  do not borrow the source novel's names. An incomplete book cannot confirm the
  full front-loading ratio. Report provisional cumulative coverage instead.
- Lead development moments and supporting-cast distribution: cite actual choices
  or realizations and distinguish manually identified beats from regex proxies.
- POV word share: identify viewpoint segments, not the most frequently mentioned
  name. Track the lead and secondary viewpoints against the book-level allocation.
- Secondary signals: -ly words, reporting verbs, combat terms, fillers and repeated
  phrases. Publish lexicons and counting definitions. A suffix count is not proof
  that every matched word is an adverb. Preserve optional/non-target distinctions.

Use reproducible counts when tooling exists. Otherwise mark a metric UNMEASURED;
never estimate a precise score by intuition. Save the method or command with the
report so later runs use the same definitions. Inspect ASR reference limitations
without discarding the owner's chosen drafting targets.

Locate meaningful departures, especially uniform sentence lengths, missing short
beats, reading level drift, dense paragraphs, late teaching or a coequal second
lead replacing the requested POV shape. Consolidate the most important repairs
with continuity findings into the existing bounded author repair. Protect exact
owner text and successful scenes. Do not iterate indefinitely to hit decimals or
hide irreconcilable targets. Bring consequential formula conflicts to the owner.

---

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — STATE_LEDGER.md

# Kindling 2680, Book Two — state ledger

> Built by TAC

Status: **Book Two entry state only. No Book Two prose exists.** Everything in sections 1–9
is the accepted Book One exit state (evening of Sunday the 16th of the tenth month, 2680),
carried from `books/book-2680-opening/STATE_LEDGER.md`,
`books/book-2680-opening/provenance/MOVEMENT-006-CONTINUITY.md`, and chapters 47–48. Where
this file and the Book One manuscript disagree, the Book One manuscript governs. Section 10
records the checks made before this file was saved. Section 11 lists what was reconciled.
Movement blocks are appended below section 11 only from surviving Book Two prose.

## 1. Calendar

Every month has thirty days and twelve months make the year (Aske's "eleven weeks and four
days" from Monday the 10th of the tenth month to the new year confirms that no day stands
between the twelfth month and the first). A month therefore begins two weekdays after the
one before it.

| Month | 1st falls on | 30th falls on | Fixed points |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tenth, 2680 | Saturday | Sunday | Book One ends Sunday the 16th. First free day of Book Two: **Monday the 17th.** |
| Eleventh, 2680 | Monday | Tuesday | **The 1st:** Rhea Sorn's card goes back to her, signed or torn. Mara's hand is on a card "until the eleventh month." |
| Twelfth, 2680 | Wednesday | Thursday | Nothing fixed. |
| First, 2681 | **Friday** | Saturday | **The 1st:** Aske's ban on anything lit ends, if he signs the card up. The night van offer begins. Perrin joins his Hall's second team. |
| Second, 2681 | Sunday | Monday | Nothing fixed. |
| Third, 2681 | **Tuesday** | **Wednesday** | **The National Measure, at Saltmere.** Selka Dray "on her feet by the third month." Rhea Sorn's card ends on the 30th. The 11th (a Friday) is seventy-seven years to the day after the Ferren keeper's fever entry of 2604. |
| Fourth, 2681 | Thursday | Friday | Nothing fixed. |

Earlier anchors that prove the chain: the fifth month's 29th was a Wednesday (the Pit); the
sixth began on a Friday; the seventh on a Sunday; the ninth on a Thursday (the Regional, Friday
the 16th and Saturday the 17th); the tenth on a Saturday.

## 2. Kiva Rowan

- **Age and household.** Seventeen. Lives over Rowan Instruments with her aunt and guardian,
  Mara Rowan. A guardian's hand is still wanted beside her own wherever a rule says "under
  eighteen." No birthday is fixed in the supplied record; Book Two does not move her age
  unless the Book One manuscript gives a date.
- **Place.** *Rowan, K., Third Division, the Crooked Line: seated. No longer provisional.
  Catcher.* (the Chair, the 14th of the tenth month). Crooked Line third team; Team Seven.
  "Her sponsor's line and every condition on her certificate stand as written. She is not
  thereby classified." There is still no column for her; there is a page headed *Witnessed*
  with two lines on it.
- **The Home Line.** *Rowan, K. Entered on the Line's roll as catcher, autumn 2680. Hand as
  before.* Not a seat. No wage but a line-hand's day rate when called. Called by a Line
  foreman, with her sponsor's leave while she has a sponsor, "to set a mark, to read ground,
  to call a count that is hers and name the ones that are not." She has not yet been called.
- **Certificate, as it stands.**
  - Orla Dane's line: recovery only, dark hands; **and one crossing, Ember to still, low, to a
    mark, declared to a count, on a sanctioned mast read by the Chair's reader, one in seven
    days.** Not certified: any stack, any third door, anything Orla has not seen six times.
  - The Chair's conditions of the spring: one door declared to the referee before every
    sanctioned event, and any change declared; her book to her sponsor weekly; no live-cell
    events until her sponsor certifies in writing; one sanctioned-event incident ends her
    season. (The 2680 season is read. Whether that last line is re-issued for 2681 is the
    Chair's to say and is not on any page.)
  - The Chair's conditions of the 2nd of the seventh month: no second door on any Circuit
    floor except a crossing declared to a count and read at the time from a sanctioned mast
    by the office's reader; a stack so read is an incident; not at the Bench; recovery only on
    any floor with a contained rift. No crossing counts unless the tape shows the cliff. Noa
    Bexley is reader to the office.
  - **Selka Dray's mast rule** (any candidate with more than one door is read from a
    sanctioned mast by a reader of the Meridian office) has gone to Saltmere under seal.
- **Crossings on the record.** Ember to still: one under a field direction (the 29th of the
  fifth month), then Orla's six on sanctioned masts, and one begun and not landed in a lane
  at the Regional. Hand to Ember: landed once (the eighth month, 0.30). A crossing costs
  about 0.30 to 0.31 of a dram, of which about 0.26 is the gap. An unlanded crossing still
  spends the hand and the week's go. A stack is a miss, forbidden, and an incident if read.
- **Three doors.** Order: hand, Ember, still ("catch, carry, plant"); 0.59 whole. **Three
  clean in all: a yard (the 29th of the eighth month), the Pit (the 17th of the ninth), the
  Old Yard (the 5th of the tenth).** Uncertified, unclassified, and by the Chair's written
  entry "not to be asked of her." The last two were done under a recorded Home Line direction
  and an officer's hand, with the price said first by her healer; the last was offered and not
  asked. Never twice inside a fortnight. Aske does not know that the arm would come back a
  fourth time.
- **Readings.** Nothing has been measured since Classification Day, 2680: Draw 3.1; Hold,
  Edge 3.0, Ward 2.6, Stance 6.2; rank unsettled (the frame flickers); first door, none; the
  Call column dark. **No rank, Hold, Draw or bench figure has been re-measured.**
- **Body.**
  - **The first two fingers of the left hand have permanently lost fine sensation.** They
    bend, grip, and report pressure ("something's pushing"). They do not tell edge from flat,
    hot from cold, or one thread from two. Ward-trace ash and rest are ruled out as remedies.
  - The left arm lost feeling to a hand above the elbow on the 5th of the tenth month and came
    back over four days "to where it was, and not a hair less."
  - **Aske's card of the 10th: nothing lit until the new year. "Not a step. Not a ward for a
    tap." Dark hands and her voice.** His standing rule before that: one crossing in seven
    days, with the hand seen the next morning; the three-door is not on his card at all.
  - The left knee is still strapped and complained once, running. The three heat-lines run
    from the breastbone over the collarbone toward the left shoulder and upper arm.
- **Handoffs she names** (the list of the 30th of the ninth month): the cord, all three turns
  (Pru Finch); the slack of a hem on her left side (she takes a right-hand corner or is called
  to); tin lids with a lip; the pin of a seal and whether wax has gone off (Anwen has the
  wax); a button and a cuff; **the count of a thing that keeps time (somebody else's, named
  before).**
- **The disk.** Cold. It warmed once, on the 29th of the fifth month, after her first correct
  deliberate crossing, and has been cold through every crossing and all three three-door
  sequences since. Recognized by the Compact Library's Reader as the plate of nine rings
  carried by the first teachers and *released* by appointment. Held of the Line; borne by K.
  Rowan; not equipment, an instrument, or evidence; no hand but the bearer's without a vote of
  the watch; the bearer writes in the fourth column within the week after any crossing. What
  it is for is unknown. The first bearer's book is lost.
- **The dream.** Occasions on record: at thirteen (Kindled, in a yard); Classification Day, 2680; **the Pit, the
  29th of the fifth month, 2680** (Book One ch. 23; that night's fever brought no dream, and she told the Pit's to
  Mara at ten, who wrote the sealed page, ch. 24). *(Corrected 2026-10-15 at the Movement Five repair: this line read
  "No recurrence since Classification Day, 2680", which Book One ch. 23 contradicts. No later recurrence is recorded
  here.)* No fever on any night of the autumn. She has Annick's phrase *the sky unroofed* and the Ferren keeper's line of 2604.
  **The name "the Unroofed Sky" has never appeared on the page.**
- **Her book.** Green cover, soft at the corners; a spring, a summer, and an autumn; no page
  ever torn out. It goes to Orla weekly and lies on the kitchen table nightly for Mara.

## 3. Team Seven

- **The four:** Kiva Rowan (catcher), Anwen Pryce (Hold), Perrin Cade (carry; calls *off*
  and keeps the brake), Kellan Renn (blade). **Jude Talley** stands as Kill by name, on the
  line for a head's share and not on the book. **Pru Finch** is a catcher with a sheet of her
  own and the standing reserve.
- **Standing.** Fourth at the Regional Measure. **Second step, in its own right;** the
  brackets came off on the 14th of the tenth month. It goes forward to the National Measure.
- **The book, signed by four on Saturday the 15th of the tenth month:**

| Line | Drams |
|---|---:|
| **Held** | **25.53** |
| **Sealed medical (W, plain ward-trace)** | **0.8** |
| By name to J. Talley, in one autumn | 7.8 |

  Check: 23.62 − 3.0 (second purse sheet and poles, for Pru) − 0.70 (call drams spent) − 0.59
  (three-door line) + 7.2 (to the book from Stopgate's pot) = 26.53; less 1.0 returned at par
  to the wardens of Stopgate = **25.53.** The loan of 4.0 to the casualty reserve went out and
  came back. Sealed: 2.0 − 1.2 (Mara Rowan's reading hand, entered as medical) = **0.8.**
- **The reserve cannot be made up.** There is no plain ward-trace to be bought in Meridian;
  every can has "had the hair" since the thaw. It "stays eight-tenths until somebody kills
  something quiet."
- **Not on the team's book:** Kiva's own weekly half dram; Pru's pay, which is off the
  Circuit's reserve card.
- **Red, and untouched:** Seven's own rung from the Pit, stepped, on Kiva's tag; Pru's bag
  from Stopgate on her own tag. Across the district 111 and 503.7 drams stand red-tagged from
  before, with 560.4 more from Stopgate. Where the red row goes is not said. The patent claim
  on the Weighbridge bag is before the Compact's court, unruled.
- **Saltmere.** **No warrant has been posted or entered.** Kellan reckons two drams a head at
  the least, five heads, ten drams. Nobody from the Circuit has been sent to the coast in
  eleven years.
- **Open inside the four:** which four Kellan stands with at Saltmere. He cut for the Halden
  license four at the Regional and declared for Seven under rule nineteen once the floor was
  struck. He counts himself among "five heads" on the 15th. No page settles it.

## 4. The offer

Rhea Sorn's card, slate blue, read in full to the four and signed on its back by all four on
Saturday the 15th of the tenth month:

> *Rowan, K. Catcher's second, to Pike. The night van, two nights in the month, from the first
> of the new year, when her healer's card is up. Dark hands. No door on my floor that is not
> on her certificate. Finch with her, for the cord, if Finch will. If T7 is called on a night
> of mine, she is T7's and I go short. Until the last of the third month. Then we look again,
> and either of us may say no. No seat is promised. R.S.*

- Kiva's two conditions, accepted: the *before* column is read out each night to all; her four
  have the first word.
- Pru Finch has said yes, on being asked by Kiva as well.
- A catcher's second "sets nothing. She calls nothing." She kneels at Pike's other hem and
  tells him afterward what he did that he did not say aloud. She is not to open her mouth
  until the bag is sealed.
- At most **six nights:** two in each of the first, second, and third months of 2681.
- The card is to go back to Rhea on **Monday the 1st of the eleventh month.**
- Rhea's slate has six columns: *Before. Closed. Lives. Works. Tinned. Remarks.*

## 5. Other people, as they stand

| Person | Body, place, and open thread |
|---|---|
| **Mara Rowan** | Grade Two, restored. Reader of the trunk for Examiner Rusk; has the hill, the bank, and the stopping of it. **Left (reading) hand starved to the wrist on the 6th and eased inside the hour: a week before she reads a drum, a month before she reads a bank.** The right hand has told her nothing since Linden Row (2677). Eleven sheets in seven columns, *How the Ferren bank was taken out of step, with what was got wrong first,* are tied and waiting on the dresser for the coast boat on Monday the 17th. Her page of the 29th (Kiva's dream as told) is sealed and timed in the Library's press. The Order's six questions are in her hands; two are to be answered. |
| **Orla Dane** | Accountable sponsor; her standing rides on Kiva's record. Holds **Jessamy Hart's card** and has shown it to nobody. Moved her crate a pace north. Bad left knee, stick. |
| **Anwen Pryce** | Has the wax and the sealed reserve. A relief's place on Doran Mace's ward line (the night van) for next summer; Mace left her name where it was (*Said so as she went*). Has told her mother. "Done minding." |
| **Perrin Cade** | **Two ribs sprung, not broken; strapped.** 380 kilos for thirty counts, cut in the stone. His Hall's second team from the new year. Minds, and says so. |
